<meta>This is the title page for a work by Valentin Weigel, published posthumously in 1700. Weigel was a 16th-century German pastor and mystic whose writings were influential in the development of radical Pietism and theosophy.</meta>

### Simple
## **Instruction**
->On<-
# **Prayer /**

### Firstly /
->**How the heart, through thor-**<-
->**ough meditation, is awakened and pre-**<-
->**pared for inward pray-**<-
->**er.**<-

### Secondly /
->**How Adam and Christ**<-
->are both within us / and not outside of us /<-
->to which all of Holy Scripture<-
->points.<- <note>Weigel emphasizes the "inner word" and the internal struggle between the fallen nature (Adam) and the divine nature (Christ) within the individual believer.</note>

->manded by Christ / even though God far<-
->anticipates us with His blessings / before<-

->Frankfurt and Leipzig / by Samuel Müller.<-

<summary>This title page introduces a mystical treatise on prayer by Valentin Weigel, focusing on the internal preparation of the heart and the spiritual presence of Adam and Christ within the soul. Published in 1700, it reflects the enduring influence of Weigel's radical spiritualist ideas over a century after his death.</summary>

<keywords>Valentin Weigel, prayer, meditation, internalism, Adam and Christ, mysticism, Zschopa, Samuel Müller, 1700</keywords>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory front matter, likely from the preface or early sections of a devotional work. The signature 'A 2' indicates this is the second leaf of the first gathering. The catchword 'lass' connects to the beginning of the next page.</meta>

# Doctor Luther in the little book
## on the Lord's Prayer says thus:

You should know that I have not spoken all these words in the prayer, for in the end, it would become a mere rattling <note>original: "Klapperwerck"; Luther uses this term to describe mindless, mechanical repetition of words without spiritual engagement, much like the sound of a wooden rattle.</note>. Rather, I wish the heart to be stirred and instructed by them as to what thoughts it should grasp in the Lord's Prayer <note>original: "Vater Unser"; the traditional German name for the Pater Noster or Lord's Prayer.</note>. For I do not bind myself to such words and syllables, yet I remain, as closely as I ever can, with the same meaning and thoughts. It often happens that in one part or petition <term>petition</term><note>The Lord's Prayer is traditionally divided into seven "petitions" or requests made to God.</note> I wander into such rich thoughts that I let the other six stand aside. And when such rich thoughts come, one should let the other prayers go...

<summary>In this excerpt, Martin Luther explains that prayer should be a heartfelt engagement rather than a mechanical recitation of words. He encourages believers to dwell on specific parts of the Lord's Prayer that stir their thoughts, even if it means not finishing the entire sequence.</summary>
<keywords>Martin Luther, Lord's Prayer, prayer practice, devotion, spontaneity, Reformation, spiritual instruction</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>The catchword "cher-" at the top of the page indicates the word "mancher" (many a one/someone) will continue on the next page. This text continues Luther’s instruction on internalizing prayer rather than rushing through liturgical repetitions.</meta>

...allow and give room to such thoughts, and listen in stillness. That is, to sit down with David <note>A reference to King David as the traditional author of the Psalms and a model of contemplative prayer.</note> and hear what the **Lord** speaks with him, and by no means hinder it. For there the **Holy Spirit** himself preaches, and one word of his preaching is better than a thousand of our **prayers**. And I have also often learned more in one **prayer** than I could have obtained from much reading and composing <note>original: "Dichten"; refers here to the intellectual effort of study or writing.</note>. Therefore, the great power lies in this: that the heart makes itself joyful and free for prayer. As **Sirach** <note>Also known as Ecclesiasticus, a book from the Apocrypha frequently quoted by Luther for its practical wisdom on the inner life.</note> also says in the 18th Chapter: **Prepare your heart before your prayer, so that you do not tempt God.** What else is it to tempt **God** than when the mouth prattles and the heart is scattered elsewhere, like that priest <note>original: "Pfaffe"; a term Luther often used derisively for clergy who performed their duties mechanically.</note> who prays in this manner: *O God, come to my assistance* <note>original Latin: "Deus in adjutorium meum intende"</note>, [then shouts to his servant] "You have hitched the horse," *O Lord, make haste to help me* <note>original Latin: "Domine ad adjuvandum me festina"</note>, [to his maid] "Maid, go there and milk the **cows**," *Glory be to the Father and the Son* <note>original Latin: "gloria patri & filio"</note>, [to his page] "Run, boy, and may the fever shake you!" <note>original: "daß dich der Ritt schut"; "Ritt" or "Rittig" was an archaic term for an ague or shivering fit.</note> *The mind intent on many things is less attentive to each single thing* <note>original Latin: "pluribus intentus minor est ad singula sensus"; a common medieval proverb regarding the limits of human concentration.</note>, for he who [many a one]...

<summary>Luther argues that the Holy Spirit teaches more through silent, focused contemplation than through rote recitation. He uses a humorous anecdote about a distracted priest to illustrate how divided attention "tempts God" and invalidates prayer.</summary>
<keywords>Martin Luther, Holy Spirit, David, Sirach, focus in prayer, Latin liturgy, clerical satire, devotion</keywords>

## for the Simple. <note>original: "für die Einfältigen"; in this context, it refers to the "simple-hearted" or common people who may lack formal theological training but possess sincere faith.</note>

living faith. But everything 
desires to be recognized and

So too this little book 
desires to be read and under-
stood by a true, believing 
person, and not by a 
<term>mouth-Christian</term> <note>original: "Maulchristen"; a derogatory term for someone who professes Christian faith with their words but does not possess it in their heart or demonstrate it in their life.</note>. Ask God 
that you may be led by the 
**Holy Spirit**; then you will 
not reject such a foundation 
of **truth**. Amen.

<summary>This concluding section of the preface asserts that spiritual truths can only be grasped by those with "living faith" rather than hypocrites. It ends with a prayer for the reader to be guided by the Holy Spirit.</summary>

<keywords>living faith, mouth-Christian, Holy Spirit, truth, spiritual discernment, hypocrisy</keywords>

## Chapter I.

# What we lose in Adam,
### we find again entirely and perfectly in Christ.

<image-desc>Large ornamental drop cap 'D' decorated with scrolling foliage and vines within a square frame.</image-desc>The first man was created by GOD from a clod of earth and made into a perfect <term>image of God</term> <note>German: "Bildniß GOttes"; the theological concept that humans were created to reflect God's own character and nature.</note> through the *breath of life* <note>original: "spiraculum vitæ"</note>, that is, through the Divine breathing-in. Thus he was endowed with perfect wisdom, just like his Father GOD; for as GOD is, so too was man—neither more nor less—only not of himself, but having received everything from GOD. And alongside this, he possessed <term>blessedness</term> <note>German: "Seeligkeit"; a state of supreme spiritual joy, peace, and divine favor.</note>; for where the wisdom of GOD is, there also is blessedness, and where blessedness is, there also is the wisdom of GOD. Now, in Paradise...

<meta>catchword: nothing (nichts)</meta>

<summary>This introductory page describes the creation of the first man, Adam, emphasizing his original state of divine wisdom and blessedness as a perfect reflection of God. It sets the stage for a discussion on how this lost perfection is regained through Jesus Christ.</summary>
<keywords>Creation, Image of God, Adam, Divine Wisdom, Blessedness, Breath of Life, Paradise</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Chapter I, transitioning from the description of Adam’s original perfection in Paradise to the consequences of the Fall and the beginning of the path to restoration through Christ. The text emphasizes the loss of the "Divine Image" and its replacement by human misery, which is only corrected by the Incarnation.</meta>

nothing was lacking in <term>Hebron</term> <note>German: "Hebron"; in certain early modern theological traditions, Hebron was identified as the site of Adam's creation or his residence in a state of grace.</note>; but after he turned away from GOD through the <term>temptation</term> <note>German: "Verführung"; literally a "leading astray," referring here to the serpent's deceit in Eden.</note> of the serpent and fell into sin, the **image of GOD** in man faded away, and he fell asleep in such wisdom; moreover, he fell and came into the power of the devil and of death, and into all misery. From this, through such <term>transgression</term> <note>German: "Ubertretung"; the act of breaking a divine law or overstepping a boundary.</note>, flesh and blood broke forth in man, and the Spirit of GOD or the Divine image was struck back; so that after the Fall, man saw nothing more than his own ruin, wretchedness, blindness, misery, and condemnation. 

But so that **Adam**—that is, all people in Adam—would not utterly perish, GOD Himself became man and let His Son become man, and be born of a woman; this same Son has shown us once again the way, the <term>blessedness</term> <note>German: "Seeligkeit"; a state of eternal happiness and divine favor.</note>, and the wisdom, through His teaching and life, as an **example** and a pattern which we should follow. For as He has redeemed us from all sins through His bitter death, He also commands that we should walk just as He walked, **1 John 2** <note>Reference to 1 John 2:6: "Whoever claims to live in him must live as Jesus did."</note>. Thus

### Chapter II.

# What great damage arises
## and follows / if one
## neglects prayer.

<summary>This page concludes a prayerful reflection on how the spiritual inheritance lost through Adam is regained through Christ, emphasizing that these gifts must be actively sought through prayer. It then introduces the second chapter, which warns of the spiritual dangers and harm caused by the neglect of prayer.</summary>
<keywords>prayer, James, Adam, Christ, repentance, spiritual inheritance, neglect of prayer, Savior</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter II regarding the dangers of neglecting prayer. It argues that failing to pray is not merely a passive oversight but a direct transgression of God's commandments, equating spiritual negligence with self-destruction.</meta>

( 5 )
<sig>A 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: truthful</meta>

The simple-hearted <note>German: *Einfältigen*; referring to those of humble, sincere, or uncomplicated faith.</note> should often reflect upon the mischief and harm that result from the neglect of prayer, so that through such contemplation they may be led to divine invocation. Firstly: whoever neglects prayer transgresses the commandment of **God** and the command of **Christ**, who commands us to pray without ceasing, to seek the Kingdom of **God**, and to knock—not for His sake, but for our own sake, so that we may become aware of the treasure and inheritance from **God**. 

Now, it is a great sin not to pray, acting against the first and second commandments: *You shall have no other gods* and *You shall not misuse the name of God*. For just as blaspheming **God**, practicing sorcery, or cursing is a great sin, it is no less a sin not to call upon **God’s** name, not to worship **God**—indeed, to not want to pray and to despise the same is as great a sin as killing or strangling oneself. <note>The author uses this stark imagery to suggest that neglecting the "breath" of prayer is a form of spiritual suicide.</note> Secondly, the neglect of prayer is a contempt and disregard for the true promise of **God**; for **God** has not only commanded us to pray, but also truly—

<vocab>Simple-hearted, Mischief, Neglect, Prayer, Invocation, Commandment, Promise, Sin</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that neglecting prayer is a serious sin that violates the first two commandments and equates the refusal to pray with spiritual self-destruction. It emphasizes that prayer is commanded by Christ for the benefit of the believer, allowing them to access their divine inheritance.</summary>
<keywords>neglect of prayer, commandments, spiritual suicide, divine invocation, inheritance, sin of omission</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 5, which ended with the assertion that God has not only commanded prayer but has also made a "truthful promise" to hear those who call upon Him.</meta>

truthful promise that He wishes to give everything for which we ask. Such a person <note>Referring back to the one who neglects prayer.</note> esteems such a promise lightly, regards it as nothing, and considers his **GOD** to be an untrustworthy man who makes a promise but does not keep it. Thirdly, this harm also arises when prayer is neglected and not practiced daily and without ceasing: that faith is weakened, diminished, and indeed even entirely lost—faith, which ought to be the power and strength of man. For we shall not overcome death and the devil with spears and cannons <note>German: *Ge-schüz*; referring to artillery or firearms, emphasizing that spiritual battles cannot be won with physical weaponry.</note>, or with bodily strength; faith in Christ must do it.

Just as the body declines and becomes powerless if it is not given its daily food and drink, even so—and much more—does faith become weary, weak, and powerless when it does not receive its nourishment, but has it withdrawn through the neglect of prayer. For through prayer, we must draw and obtain the power of faith; and that is wisdom or eternal life. Fourthly, through the omission of prayer, the Holy Spirit departs from the

<summary>This page describes how neglecting prayer treats God's promises as worthless and leads to the starvation of one's faith. The author compares prayer to daily food for the soul, arguing that without it, a person lacks the spiritual strength to overcome death and the devil.</summary>
<keywords>prayer, faith, promises of God, spiritual strength, Holy Spirit, spiritual nourishment</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of consequences resulting from the neglect of prayer. The previous page ended mid-sentence, stating that through the omission of prayer, the Holy Spirit departs from the human being.</meta>

humans; thus, through the deprivation of this light, he becomes blind, walks in darkness, and can neither recognize himself nor see his **GOD**. God's will remains unknown to him; he robs himself of **GOD** and His kingdom. And because he has no light to recognize God’s will, he must suffer great blows in times of spiritual trial <term>Anfechtung: a profound state of spiritual struggle, temptation, or a sense of being abandoned by God</term> and often despairs entirely. But where the Holy Spirit and faith are present, the world would also be overcome (1 John 5).

Fifthly, from the neglect of prayer there comes and follows a secure <note>In this context, "secure" refers to "carnal security"—a dangerous state of spiritual complacency or overconfidence.</note>, bold, and wicked life, prepared for all vices and shames. Such a person falls from one sin into another and does not feel how deeply he is stuck within them; he opens door and window to the **Devil**. Even when **GOD** imparts to him the goods of this world—wife, child, health, wealth—because he does not pray, he imagines that these things happen to him by chance, or that he obtains them through his own diligence and labor without **GOD**, and thus becomes ungrateful toward his **Creator**.

Sixthly, since man, after the Fall, lives in great danger of body and soul, he also throws himself into the power of the **devils**, of the evil

<sig>A 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: spirits</meta>

<summary>This page outlines the spiritual blindness and moral decay that follow the neglect of prayer, leading to a life of complacency and ingratitude. The author warns that without prayer, one attributes worldly success to luck or effort rather than to God, leaving the soul vulnerable to the devil.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual blindness, Anfechtung, carnal security, ingratitude, spiritual trial, devil, providence, the Fall</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the sixth point regarding the dangers of neglecting prayer, specifically focusing on the vulnerability of the soul to spiritual and human enemies. It then introduces the seventh point regarding the futility and anxiety of a life lived without divine guidance.</meta>

spirits under heaven, and of all evil people, who like the **devils** also lie in wait for the godly, openly and secretly, to destroy them. And whoever lives without prayer is driven by such misfortune like a ship by the waves of the tempestuous sea, and has no protection, help, nor comfort against such danger. Seventhly, whoever neglects prayer must undoubtedly be the most wretched in all his conduct and life, standing always in anxiety and fear, uncertain of his affairs, and without doubt completely doubtful as to how his undertaking will reach an end; with toil and labor he seeks, and finds only <term>Stückwerck: piecemeal or fragmentary work; efforts that are incomplete or lack a solid foundation because they are not blessed by God</term>, and in the end, it cannot turn out well. The Scripture says indeed that it goes well for the **godless**—yes, it goes well "backwards" <note>This is a sarcastic idiomatic expression suggesting that their "success" is actually a form of spiritual regression or impending ruin.</note>—they flourish for a while, but before you look around, they are no longer there:

### Chapter III.
# That Man Obtains Great Benefit
## and Profit When He Stays
### Constant in Prayer.

Since the Fall <note>Referring to the original sin of Adam and Eve in the Garden of Eden.</note> and his disobedience, man has become entirely lazy and sluggish toward all divine matters. Therefore, so that he does not remain or perish in such a state, he should rouse and awaken himself through prayer and various contemplations; and here he should consider the great benefit and profit <note>original: "Nuß und Frommen"; early modern German terms for utility and spiritual advantage.</note> of holy prayer. First, he who prays diligently follows the command and law of God, which He commanded through Christ in Matthew 7: **Pray, seek, knock**; <note>A reference to Matthew 7:7: "Ask, and it shall be given you; seek, and ye shall find; knock, and it shall be opened unto you."</note> he calls upon his God and Father, honors the one true God, and does not invent any foreign gods for himself. Secondly, he who practices prayer does not despise God’s faithful assurance and promise, nor does he hold them in low esteem; rather, he makes it clearly known in his heart that divine promises are to be held in high and worthy regard,

<meta>catchword: Likewise/</meta>

<summary>This page introduces Chapter III, arguing that prayer is a necessary remedy for the spiritual laziness caused by the Fall and a way to honor God's commands and promises.</summary>
<keywords>The Fall, spiritual laziness, Matthew 7, divine commands, divine promises, honoring God, benefits of prayer</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 10, which concluded by discussing how prayer honors God's promises. This page continues the enumeration of the specific benefits and spiritual fruits of a constant prayer life.</meta>

Likewise, [it shows] that **GOD** is truthful, and that He neither can nor will lie. Thirdly, through prayer, faith is not diminished or extinguished; rather, it increases and grows daily like a tree. In this faith stands all our power, comfort, and strength against all enemies and adversity; indeed, it is our victory (1 John 5) <note>Referring to 1 John 5:4: "For whatsoever is born of God overcometh the world: and this is the victory that overcometh the world, even our faith."</note> which also overcomes the world; and through prayer, we resist the unbelievers who wish us harm. Fourthly, if we pray diligently without ceasing, we receive the Holy Spirit; that is, we give Him room and a place to dwell within us; He remains with us and governs within us (John 14) <note>A reference to Christ's promise of the Holy Spirit in John 14:16–17.</note>. For we are awakened in the true light and knowledge of **GOD**, so that we rightly understand **GOD’s** will and remain in the Kingdom of **GOD**, partaking in all heavenly blessings. Fifthly, through constant prayer, complacency <note>original: "Sicherheit"; in early modern theology, this refers to "carnal security," a dangerous state of spiritual overconfidence or apathy where one feels beyond the reach of temptation.</note> is prevented, and sin is resisted—which otherwise would break forth into deeds from our flesh and blood. We walk with a joyful conscience, and this is a blessed knighthood <note>original: "seelige Ritterschafft"; a common metaphor for the "Christian warfare" or the spiritual struggle to maintain faith against the world, the flesh, and the devil.</note>: that we maintain a good conscience and faith. To

Sixth: a person must be prepared for great **temptation** <note>original: "Anfechtung"; a specific Lutheran term referring to deep spiritual trials, doubt, or the feeling of being abandoned by God.</note>, danger, and misery; but through inward prayer, the **Devil** and all the temptations of all wicked people are resisted. For prayer is a strong tower against all our enemies; **GOD** is our firm fortress <note>A likely allusion to Psalm 46 and Martin Luther’s famous hymn, "A Mighty Fortress Is Our God."</note> in all our needs. And even if an intrusion were made through the craftiness of the **Devil** or of wicked people, it must all turn out for the best; for while things may often happen to the godly for their own good, for the ungodly they happen seven times more, and always for the worst.

Seventh: from inward and constant prayer, it also follows that one can always rejoice in the **Holy Spirit**, with gratitude toward **GOD**, according to the teaching of **St. Paul**, 1 Thessalonians 5: *Rejoice always, pray without ceasing, give thanks in all circumstances* <note>original: "Semper gaudere, incessanter orate, in omnibus gratias agite"</note>, for this is the will of **GOD** for you. No displeasure, worry, or sadness arises from prayer; rather, nothing but joy and delight, because of the lovely and friendly conversation with the eternal **GOD**. For once prayer is finished, one is certain that their affairs will reach a happy conclusion,

# That a true Christian should
# much rather walk the narrow way in Christ
# than the broad way in
# Adam.

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the opening of Chapter IV, which discusses the choice between the narrow way of Christ and the broad way of Adam. It expands on the theological concept of free will and the biblical metaphor of the two paths.</meta>

( 14 )

Scripture says that **Adam** was placed in the middle of **Paradise**, between **time** and **eternity**; that is, he might strive for the **eternal** above him in the **narrow way**, or for the **temporal** below him in the **broad way**. Thus, life and death, light and darkness, were set before man. As it happened then with **Adam**, so it is still. For after the Fall comes **Christ**, and he directs us away from **Adam**, out of the **broad way**, and toward himself into the **narrow way**, without any compulsion. For he wishes to force no one toward either damnation or **salvation**; you are free to reach out for whichever you desire, through the **prevenient grace** <note>original: *fürlauffende Gnade*; this is a theological term for grace that "comes before" or precedes human decision, enabling the will to choose God</note> which is denied to no person, whether young or old. Now **Christ** our **Lord** and **Savior**, **Mediator** and **Redeemer**, says: *Enter through the narrow gate* (**Matthew**, chapter 7); for the gate is wide and the way is broad that leads to damnation, and there are many who walk upon it; and the gate is narrow and the way is small <note>original: *schmahl*; meaning narrow or constricted</note> that leads to life, and there are few who find it. From these words, it is clearly found that there are still two ways, one

### Chapter V.

# What a Person / before beginning to pray / should chiefly consider / so that their heart may be directed upward to God.

In order that we simple and beginning <note>original: *anhebenden*; refers to those in the early stages of spiritual discipline or the "purgative" way.</note> people may arrive at inward prayer in spirit and in truth / which God alone requires and desires; we take it upon ourselves to explain several preliminary considerations / and these are, first: That God knows all things beforehand / better than we what is necessary for us / before we ask. Second / that God entices, incites, and drives all people / and exhorts them to pray / and also promises a certain hearing <note>original: *Erhörung*; the act of God listening to and granting a petition.</note> of prayer. Third / that **GOD** is no respecter of persons / He loves them all equally. Fourth / that it is just as great a sin to want to pray on account of some real or supposed worthiness or holiness / as it is to neglect one’s prayer on account of hidden sin. Fifth / that one does not need to run after **God** to a certain place / but—

## The VI. Chapter.

## Chapter VII.

# That God lures, incites, and admonishes all people to prayer, and also promises a true hearing to all, and to what end such preliminary contemplation serves.

Before a person begins to say their Our Father <note>The "Our Father" refers to the Lord's Prayer, the central prayer in Christian tradition</note>, they should also first well consider this teaching: God lures, incites, drives, and admonishes all people to prayer, and He also certainly wills to hear them. ## Chapter VIII.

### That GOD is no respecter of persons / but loves them all equally / and what purpose such preliminary contemplation serves.

I have been sufficiently convinced until now / that our Father in heaven hears, knows, and sees all things better beforehand / than a person can pray or think. Likewise / I am also certain / that GOD has commanded everyone to pray / and has also promised and pledged a true hearing to all who worship / that He will hear them: Only in this do I now have a doubt / whether He also wants to hear *me* / whether He also looks upon me / and this is no small hindrance in many unpracticed hearts <note>The author uses "unpracticed" to refer to those new to spiritual discipline or meditation.</note> / that they think / GOD does not love them as much as other people / that He is a respecter of persons <note>original: "Anseher der Personen." This is a biblical idiom (Acts 10:34) meaning God does not show favoritism based on status or identity.</note> / that He chooses one before the other / and for this purpose they use the saying of Paul drawn from the Prophet in Romans 9: *Esau have I hated / and Jacob have I loved* / etc. <note>Romans 9:13. This verse was a central point of debate in the Reformation regarding predestination and God's sovereign choice.</note> But against such a hindrance and spiritual trial <note>original: "Anfechtung," a key term in German theology referring to a crisis of faith, doubt, or spiritual assault.</note>—

### That all human worthiness
### does not advance prayer, and that
### past sins, forgiven in Christ,

### prayer in any way.

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter X, addressing the common feeling of unworthiness that hinders prayer. It picks up from the catchword "helps" (hilfft) on the previous page, which concluded the thought that human piety does not "help" one's standing before God.</meta>

<page-num>( 43 )</page-num>

Because it commonly occurs to a person that they are sinful, unworthy, unholy, and unjust, and that for this reason God will not hear them in the same way He heard Elijah, Daniel, or Abraham, it is necessary that they practice this preliminary meditation <term>Vorbetrachtung</term> <note>original: "Vorbetrachtung." A spiritual exercise or mental preparation intended to set one's mindset before beginning formal prayer.</note> and speak thus in their heart: "Just as my own worthiness or piety helps nothing toward being heard in prayer—as Christ must do everything within me, even if I were Elijah or Daniel myself—so too my past sins and unworthiness do me no harm. Christ has taken everything away, even if I were the greatest sinner or the murderer on the cross <note>original: "Mörder am Creuze." A reference to the "Penitent Thief" crucified alongside Jesus, who was granted entry into Paradise despite his crimes.</note> himself." For there is indeed no condemnation for those who remain in Christ Jesus, who no longer walk according to the flesh, but according to the Spirit, as it is written in Romans 8. God also testifies through the prophet that it shall not harm the repentant person that they were once unjust, as seen in Ezekiel 18.

It is believed by the simple-minded that the Fathers <note>Refers to the biblical patriarchs and prophets such as Abraham, Elijah, and Daniel mentioned earlier.</note> were heard because of their own holiness, but this delusion is false. By it, honor is taken away from Christ and attributed to man. Just as little as the moon, with its own shine, helps the sun

<summary>This page argues that the efficacy of prayer depends entirely on Christ's merit rather than the individual's holiness or past sins. It reassures the reader that even great biblical figures were heard by God through grace, not their own worthiness.</summary>

<keywords>prayer, worthiness, Elijah, Daniel, Abraham, Christ's merit, repentance, Romans 8, Ezekiel 18, grace</keywords>

Since all gifts are already there beforehand, as contained in the **Our Father**, <note>original: "Vater unser." This refers to the Lord's Prayer, which the author views as a summary of all divine gifts.</note> so the forgiveness of sins is also a gift of **God** and is present beforehand, as far as **God** and His gracious mercy are concerned; for **God** comes to us with all His gifts far, far beforehand, even before we pray, and yet they are not recognized without prayer. When you pray, *forgive us our debt, as we forgive our debtors*, you should know this interpretation: namely, that in this petition you are awakened and reminded of three things. First, when you say, *forgive us our debt, as we forgive*, etc., you pray in this way:

# Chapter XI.

## That a true worshiper <term>Anbeter: one who prays or worships</term> does not need to run out into the field to a specific place, but finds Him everywhere in spirit and in truth.

When you wish to pray, repeat this brief preliminary meditation <term>Vorbetrachtung: a spiritual preparation or introductory reflection</term> in this manner:

> Dear **God** and **Father**, I am now sufficiently informed how you see, know, and hear all things better, long before I begin to pray; yes, I also know that you drive, entice, and lure me, and exhort me for my own benefit and piety. Likewise, that you are no respecter of persons <note>A biblical idiom (Acts 10:34) meaning God does not show favoritism based on status or origin.</note>; you love, create, and redeem them all equally. From all this, I find that I do not need to awaken or rouse you through my crying and praying—you who throughout all eternity neither slumber nor sleep—but rather I must use it to remind, awaken, and rouse myself, so that I may learn to recognize how you know all things, how you are so kind, and how you run ahead of all people with your grace <note>original: "Gnade zuvor lauffest," referring to the theological concept of prevenient grace—the idea that God's grace is already active in a person's life before they even seek Him.</note>, and offer your hand,

penetrates all spirits, however pure and clean they may be: **GOD** is always present to us, but we are not always present to Him; that is, we who have turned away <note>The author uses the term "Abgekehrten," referring to the mystical concept of *aversio*, where the human soul turns its attention away from the Divine toward worldly things.</note> do not perceive the Divine Presence, just as a dim-sighted <note>original: "Blingender." ...how the same <note>Referring back to the Kingdom of God mentioned in the broader context of Luke 17.</note> is not outside of us, but is within us, **Luke 17**. **Paul** also speaks this way: what business is it of mine to judge those who are outside? <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 5:12, where Paul distinguishes between the community of believers and those outside it.</note> This "outside" or "inside" should not be calculated according to a physical spot or location, but according to faith in the Spirit or the inner person—*not by reason of place* <note>original: "non ratione loci"</note>. For if one were to understand it according to a physical, visible location—in the way the **Antichrist** <note>In this context, "Antichrist" likely refers to institutional religious structures that the author believes have corrupted the spiritual nature of the Gospel by tying it to specific earthly locations or hierarchies.</note> binds the **Kingdom of God**—then Christians would have to leave the world entirely. Christians do not divide or separate themselves from anyone, for they are neither sectarian nor partisan; rather, the world separates and divides itself from the Christians, which they must allow to happen with great sorrow. The location or spot makes one neither blessed nor damned. The reason for this is: if a physical location could make one blessed, then **Lucifer** would never have become a devil, for he was in a blessed place, so to speak—namely, in **Heaven**. Likewise, **Adam** would not have fallen into "death-blindness" <note>The state of spiritual ignorance and separation from God resulting from the Fall.</note> or sin, for he was in **Paradise**. Conversely, if the location were to damn a person, then no human could be saved, since we are all in the world under the **Devil’s kingdom**, who is a prince of this world. ## Chapter XVI.

# That Adam and the Forbidden
# Tree are Within Us, and No Longer
# Outside of Us.

It is not enough to recognize and know from the **letter of the Scripture** <note>The "letter of the Scripture" refers to a literal or intellectual understanding of the Bible that lacks personal, spiritual application.</note> that there are two important persons in the world, **Adam** and **Christ**, or two trees in **Paradise**—one of death—or two seeds—the **serpent's seed** and the **woman's seed** <note>A reference to Genesis 3:15, traditionally interpreted as the perpetual struggle between the influence of the Devil and the lineage of the Messiah (Christ).</note>—and the like: Rather, I must also necessarily see and recognize that all of this is within the human being and by no means outside of him, and how it enters into the human being and dwells therein. For no one ascends into heaven except he who has come from heaven, namely the **Son of Man** <note>Original: *Menschen Sohn*. A title used by Jesus (referencing John 3:13) to describe his divine origin and humanity.</note>, as seen in the 3rd chapter of John. Likewise, no one descends into **damnation** <note>Original: *Verdamniß*. In this mystical context, damnation is often viewed as a present state of spiritual death or separation from God within the soul.</note> except he alone who is already in damnation, namely the **old Adam**.

he received all divine wisdom; the Word of God was within him, the Law of God was within him, the Kingdom of God was within him. At this point, a simple, untrained person might think and say / since the **Kingdom of God** is in all people (**Luke 17**) / as is the **Word of God** (**Romans 10**) / and the **Law of God** / will not all people then be saved / because they all alike carry such a treasure in their **vessels** <note>vessels: A biblical metaphor (cf. 2 Corinthians 4:7) for the human body or soul which, though fragile and "earthen," can contain the glory of God.</note>? 

**Answer:** No / it does not follow from that / God and His Kingdom are in them / but the ungodly are not in God and His Kingdom / they are indeed "apprehended and known" by God (**Galatians 4** / **Philippians 3**) / but they do not recognize or grasp God / in the way that they are known and apprehended: God loves all His enemies / and goes out to meet them <note>original: *kommt ihnen zuvor*. This refers to "prevenient grace," the belief that God’s love reaches out to humans before they have even turned toward Him.</note> / like the **impartial sun** <note>A reference to Matthew 5:45, noting that God "makes his sun rise on the evil and on the good."</note> / but it is of no help / if they do not, in return, [have/love] God <note>The text breaks off mid-sentence; the catchword "ha-" suggests the next word is "haben" (to have or to love).</note>

<summary>This page explains that while the ungodly live in peace with the Devil, Christians face an internal struggle to suppress their fallen nature. It clarifies that although God's presence and grace are offered to all like the sun, salvation requires an active, reciprocal response from the individual.</summary>

### Chapter XIX.

## That our Savior Jesus Christ has briefly enclosed and contained all natural and supernatural goods of the body and the soul within the seven petitions of the Lord's Prayer / to show / how the impartial **GOD** and Father / anticipates all people with all such goods / before they even pray.

->**Chapter XXII.**<-

## Explanation that we must, through the “Our Father,” remind and exhort ourselves of the treasure currently present / and also through such practice preserve the recognized treasure. Our Father, who art in heaven, hallowed be thy name, etc.

Christ our Savior <note>original: *Seeligmacher*. Literally "one who makes one blessed," a common Baroque-era term for Christ as the Bringer of Salvation.</note> has prescribed and commanded the holy prayer for us / not so that we should obtain something new or foreign from **GOD** through it / but so that we might stir ourselves up and exhort ourselves regarding the treasure currently present / which is given to us / before we even pray: For everything for which we ask / is already there. No one, however, may obtain, know, find, or rightly enjoy the same / unless he prays for it in this way / Our Father / who art in heaven / etc. All such things are already there / and **GOD** is a Father of the believers / but he is not *your* Father / so long as you do not pray: Therefore, when you pray with heart and mouth / you make **GOD** your dearest Father / and honor the one **GOD** in three Persons. Secondly, [you] ack-

<summary>This page introduces the Lord's Prayer not as a way to ask God for new gifts, but as a spiritual exercise to recognize and "activate" the divine treasure already placed within the believer.</summary>

nowledge that all people are your brothers, whom you should love equally; for as often as you speak the gracious word "Father," you acknowledge three Persons in the Godhead <term>Godhead: The divine nature or essence of God, often used in theological discussions of the Trinity.</term>, for you cannot speak the word "Father" unless you are a child or son in the Son **Jesus Christ**, since no one comes to the Father except through the Son and with the Son: but just as little as one comes to the Father without the Son, just as little does one come to Christ without the Spirit of the Father and of the Son; for who would want to confess Christ as a **Lord** without the Holy Spirit? Indeed, one must first hear and learn it from the heavenly Father if one is to come to the Son, and pray in the Son, or in the name of **Jesus**. See, every believer has, through his prayer, the one true **God** in three Persons; whereas he who does not pray may well have a Creator, but he has no Father; and because he has no Father, he is also no child or son in the Son; thus he denies the Father and the Son, as well as the Holy Spirit; and because he is no child or son, he remains a servant <note>original: *Knecht*. and bastard <term>bastard: original "Banckhart," an archaic term for an illegitimate child, derived from the idea of a child conceived on a bench (Bank) rather than in a marriage bed.</term>: Therefore, he uses all the goods in this life not as a legitimate child, but as a child of a whore and a bastard. ## Chapter XXV.

**Even though the forgiveness of**
**sins is prepared for all people beforehand in Christ,**
**nevertheless no one may obtain**
**absolution <note>The formal release from guilt or the declaration of forgiveness.</note> unless he then prays for**
**it: forgive us our debt, as**
**we forgive our**
**debtors.**

Through this petition, every Christian may remind themselves of three things: First, that GOD out of pure grace has previously covered all sins and forgiven the believers before they ask, and yet no one becomes aware of such a thing without prayer. Secondly, that everyone is obliged to forgive his fellow man <note>original: "Neben-Menschen," literally the person standing next to you; one's neighbor.</note> all faults, great and small, many or few; for as the Father does, so also shall the son do. Whoever knows the true understanding of this petition, and can use it rightly, finds absolution <note>The formal declaration of forgiveness from sins.</note> and the priest within himself, whoever he may be. The forgiveness of sins does not depend on our confessing or praying, but on the pure grace of God through Christ; and such grace precedes all things, not waiting until we become worthy or capable. But no one will ever become a partaker of such gracious forgiveness of Christ, or through Christ, unless he then prays: *Forgive us our debt, as we forgive*, etc. Thus we now also pray for that which we already have, although to the unpracticed it is incomprehensible and hard to believe. Therefore the simple-minded <note>original: "Einfältigen," referring to those who are spiritually or theologically uneducated.</note> might think and say: "If absolution is already present, then I need not pray for it; I need not go to confession; I need not partake of the Lord’s Supper <note>original: "Nachtmahl," referring to the Eucharist or Holy Communion.</note>." ### Chapter XXVI.

## Since everyone is their own greatest friend / and all evil comes from within us / so Christ teaches us to pray: Lead us not into temptation / but deliver us from evil.

That everyone should guard and protect themselves against the Devil and the world is common knowledge to all; but that one should guard against himself more than against the Devil is a marvelous thing to the unpracticed <note>original: "Ungeübten." This refers to those who are new to the spiritual life or have not yet developed deep self-awareness through meditation.</note> / and almost difficult to believe. But spiritually wise people <note>original: "Gottweise Menschen." A term used for those who possess a wisdom derived from a deep, mystical relationship with God rather than worldly learning.</note> find it to be very true / that man has no greater enemy than himself / and whoever knows himself must testify to it. There is nothing so difficult as overcoming oneself. The great heroes have conquered many lands and peoples / but themselves they could not master / as we read of Hercules, Hector, Samson, Solomon, and Alexander the Great <note>original: "Hercule, Hectore, Samfone, Salomone und Alexandro Magno." The author lists these classical and biblical figures as examples of external strength coupled with internal moral or spiritual weakness.</note> : And just as overcoming oneself / and commanding oneself / is the greatest thing / so it is also the most necessary for a

<vocab>Self-conquest, Christ, Temptation, Evil, Devil, Spiritually wise people, Hercules, Hector, Samson, Solomon, Alexander the Great</vocab>

Men <note>Catchword for the following page, likely beginning the word "Menschen" (person/man).</note>

<summary>This chapter introduces the petition "Lead us not into temptation" by arguing that the human ego and self-love are more dangerous enemies than the Devil or the world. It cites famous historical and biblical figures who could conquer nations but failed to master their own internal desires.</summary>

man, as the wise Solomon <note>original: "Salomon." Referring to the biblical King Solomon, traditionally associated with wisdom and the Book of Proverbs.</note> testifies. If you wish to be a king, see, I will show you a kingdom: rule yourself. Therefore, Christ considers nothing so necessary as self-denial and the renouncing <note>original: "Verzeihung." In this early modern context, the word carries the sense of renunciation or "giving up" one's own will, alongside its modern meaning of forgiveness.</note> of oneself, so that everyone should renounce himself, let go of himself, hate his own soul and life, and completely die away from himself (John 12, Matthew 16, Luke 9, 14). > Lead us not into temptation, but deliver us [from evil], etc.

Let no one say that he is **tempted** by **GOD**; for **GOD** is not a tempter of evil, he tempts no one. Rather, each person is tempted when he is lured and enticed by his own desire; then, after desire has conceived, it gives birth to sin; but sin, when it is finished, gives birth to death <note>original: "Niemand spreche / daß er von GOTT versu-chet werde..." In a theological sense, this refers to apostasy or the turning of the soul away from the divine toward the material self.</note> or turning from him toward ourselves—so that we fall into various trials <term>Anfechtung: a specific type of spiritual struggle or "assault" on one's faith</term> and temptation—he allows us to be exhorted and reminded through this petition that we should stand on the **sentry duty** <note>original: "Schildtwache." A military metaphor common in post-Reformation spirituality, urging the believer to remain as vigilant as a guard on a watchtower.</note> and not become complacent. Therefore we pray: "Lead us not into temptation," that is: teach us to consider how you do not tempt anyone at all, nor do you have any pleasure in our straying from you and thus plunging ourselves into various trials; therefore help us, so that we remain in you alone and cling to you, and do not thus stray from you and tempt ourselves. # Closing Prayer

O eternal, kind **GOD** and **Lord**, dear Father, I thank you from the bottom of my heart that you not only deliver me from evil and lead me into no temptation, but also paternally remind and exhort me through this prayer how all evil and trial <term>Anfechtung: a term used in German theology to describe a deep spiritual crisis, trial, or "dark night of the soul" where one feels abandoned by God</term> come from within myself and not from you. 

Therefore, when I turn toward the external man in **Adam** <note>In the theology of this period, "Adam" represents the "old self" or the fallen, sinful nature of humanity.</note>—that is, when I do not pray: *Lead us not into temptation, but deliver us, etc.*—I soon begin to think that you are a tempter toward evil and that you bring all plagues and misery upon mankind. I also tend to regard the **Devil** as a much greater enemy than I am to myself; and to support this, I make use of the literal sense of the Scripture: *Your adversary the **Devil** walks about* <note>original: "Euer Widersacher der Teuffel gehet herum" — a reference to 1 Peter 5:8, which describes the devil as a roaring lion.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the commentary on the Lord's Prayer with a personal closing prayer. It emphasizes that the greatest source of spiritual trial is the self (the "old Adam") rather than external forces or God Himself.</summary>
<keywords>Closing Prayer, Adam, spiritual trial, temptation, self-reflection, Devil, Lord's Prayer</keywords>

Instead, all temptation and evil come from within ourselves, because we willfully turn away from what is good.
